--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of the champion:**
The champion uses 2 states:
- State 0: GoToColor (clr=4=yellow) - moves toward yellow objects
- State 1: GoAwayColor (clr=4=yellow) - moves away from yellow objects

This is interesting - yellow (clr=4) doesn't seem to be a wall color mentioned. The transitions use floor color (GRAY=1) and random probability conditions. The robot seems to be doing something useful by going toward/away from yellow.

Wait, let me reconsider the color mapping. The colors available are {1,2,3,4,5,6}. The arena has RED walls on nest side, BLUE and GREEN blocks behind source halves. So:
- clr=1: red (nest wall)
- clr=2: green (one source half wall)  
- clr=3: blue (other source half wall)
- clr=4: yellow (robot LEDs?)
- clr=5: magenta
- clr=6: cyan

The champion goes toward yellow (other robots with yellow LEDs?) and away from yellow. This seems to create a foraging pattern.

**Key insight:** The score decreases (good) when robots bring items from ONE half, and increases (bad) from the other. We want robots to:
1. Go to the correct source (BLUE or GREEN - we don't know which is good)
2. Return to nest (WHITE floor, RED wall)

**Strategy for new machines:**
- Try going toward RED (nest wall) when carrying items, toward BLUE or GREEN when not
- Use floor colors (BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=middle) for navigation

**Machine designs:**

1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploitation variants**: Tweak champion parameters slightly
3. **Exploration**: Try 3-state machines with explicit foraging logic:
   - State: Explore/go to source (BLACK floor)
   - State: At source, go back to nest (WHITE floor via RED wall)
   - Use floor color transitions

Let me design 14 machines:

1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Champion with tweaked probabilities
3. Champion with clr=3 (blue - one source)
4. Champion with clr=2 (green - other source)
5. 3-state: explore → source → nest
6. 3-state with GoToColor red for homing
7. 2-state with different parameters
8. 2-state GoToColor blue/GoToColor red
9. 3-state foraging with neighbor conditions
10. 2-state with clr=1 (red wall for nest)
11. 3-state mix
12. 2-state exploitation variant
13. 3-state exploration
14. 4-state exploration

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 2:**

The top performers all share a clear pattern:
- State 0: GoToColor (clr=4, yellow) - moving toward yellow (other robots' LEDs?)
- State 1: GoAwayColor (clr=4, yellow) - moving away from yellow
- Transitions from State 0 use: GRAY floor (C=1) with low probability, random (C=5) with low probability, and neighbor-count conditions (C=3)
- Transitions from State 1 use: GRAY floor (C=1) with high probability, random (C=5) with moderate probability

The champion uses: GoToColor yellow → [on gray with p=0.13, random p=0.22, neighbors>7 w=14, neighbors>6 w=17] → GoAwayColor yellow → [on gray with p=0.95, random p=0.35] → back

This seems to create a foraging pattern where robots cluster near the source (using yellow LEDs to find each other), then disperse. The GRAY floor transition likely triggers when returning from source/nest areas.

**Interpretation:**
- GoToColor yellow: robots aggregate near each other (swarm at source area)
- When on GRAY (returning path): switch to GoAwayColor yellow (disperse, head back)
- The neighbor thresholds prevent overcrowding

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Vary the gray floor probability in state 0 (currently 0.13 - try 0.10-0.20)
- Vary the random probability in state 0 (currently 0.22 - try 0.15-0.30)
- Vary the neighbor thresholds
- Try adding a WHITE floor transition to help robots know when at nest
- Vary state 1 transitions

**Exploration:**
- Try 3-state versions with an explicit third state
- Try different color targets
- Try using WHITE/BLACK floor conditions more explicitly

## Reasoning

**Key observations:**

The champion (-3.17) and top performers all share the same 2-state topology:
- State 0: GoToColor(yellow, clr=4), LEDs off
- State 1: GoAwayColor(yellow, clr=4), LEDs off
- State 0 transitions: GRAY floor (p≈0.13), random (p≈0.22), neighbors>7 (w≈14), neighbors>6 (w≈17)
- State 1 transitions: GRAY floor (p≈0.95), random (p≈0.35)

The 3-state variants performed poorly. The LED color matters - yellow LEDs broke it (score 0.02), but LEDs off works well.

**What's happening:** Robots with LEDs off use yellow to find each other (other robots' LEDs are off=0, not yellow). Wait - clr=4 is yellow but LEDs are off (cle=0). So they're not tracking each other's LEDs. They must be tracking something else yellow in the environment... or perhaps "yellow" targets are absent and the GoToColor/GoAwayColor behavior degenerates into something useful like random exploration with biases.

Actually, with no yellow objects in the arena, GoToColor(yellow) with no targets visible might behave like exploration, while GoAwayColor(yellow) might behave differently. The floor/neighbor transitions switch between these modes creating an effective foraging pattern.

**Exploitation focus:**
- Fine-tune the champion's parameters: gray probability (0.13), random probability (0.22), neighbor thresholds (7,6) and steepness (14,17)
- Try adding WHITE floor transitions to state 1 (helps at nest)
- Try adding BLACK floor transitions to state 0 (helps at source)
- The best performer #1 added WHITE floor (p=0.85) to state 1 and got -2.88
- The best performer #2 added BLACK+WHITE to state 1 and got -2.88

**New ideas:**
- Try varying steepness parameters more
- Try neighbor threshold of 8 in state 0
- Try lower random probability in state 1
- Try adding a BLACK floor condition to state 0 (triggers when at source)

## Reasoning

**Observations from Rounds 2-5:**

The champion (-3.17) remains unchanged. The core 2-state topology is clearly optimal:
- State 0: GoToColor(yellow), LEDs off, 4 transitions: GRAY(p=0.13), random(p=0.22), neighbors>7(w=14), neighbors>6(w=17)
- State 1: GoAwayColor(yellow), LEDs off, 2-3 transitions: GRAY(p=0.95), random(p=0.35)

Key findings from recent rounds:
- Adding neighbors>5(w=12) to state 1 gives -3.12 (best this round)
- Adding neighbors>3(w=8) to state 1 gives -3.00
- Adding neighbors>4(w=10) to state 1 gives -2.83 (already tried many times)
- Using WHITE floor in state 1 transition hurts when combined with neighbor condition
- Using BLACK floor in state 1 as first condition hurts
- Using BELOW-neighbor condition (C=4) in state 0 slightly worse

**Key insight:** The neighbor threshold in state 1 matters. neighbors>5(w=12) is best so far. Let me explore:
- Different steepness values for neighbors>5 condition in state 1
- Try neighbors>5 with varying steepness (w=10, 11, 13, 14, 15)
- Try neighbors>6(w=12) in state 1
- Try neighbors>4(w=12) in state 1
- Try combining neighbors>5(w=12) with another condition
- Try varying state 0 parameters slightly while keeping state 1 with neighbors>5(w=12)

**Exploration:**
- Try reducing random probability in state 0 further (0.10, 0.12)
- Try different neighbor thresholds in state 0
- Try a version where state 1 has neighbors below threshold (C=4)

## Reasoning

**Key observations from all rounds:**

The champion (-3.17) persists. Interesting new data:
- Best this round: 4 transitions in state 1 with neighbors>5(w=12) + WHITE(p=0.85) gives -3.15
- p0x0=0.11 (lower gray prob in state 0) + neighbors>5(w=12) in state 1 gives -3.08
- The champion itself (2 transitions in state 1) scored -0.85 this round (stochastic!)
- neighbors>6(w=12) in state 1 hurts (-1.32)
- neighbors>4(w=12) in state 1 hurts (-1.27)
- **Confirmed sweet spot**: neighbors>5(w=12) in state 1

**Key insight**: The scores are quite stochastic - the champion scored -0.85 in one run vs -3.17 in another. The "true" best might be around -3.5 or lower with better parameters.

**Strategy for Round 7:**
- Focus on neighbors>5(w=12) variant with 4 transitions in state 1 (best this round: -3.15)
- Vary WHITE floor probability (0.80, 0.82, 0.88, 0.90)
- Try different steepness for neighbors>5 in state 1 (w=11, 13)
- Vary gray probability in state 0 (0.11, 0.12, 0.13)
- Try 4-transition state 1 with BLACK floor instead of WHITE floor
- Try combining neighbors>5(w=12) with random condition instead of WHITE floor
- Explore: try state 0 with only 3 transitions (drop one neighbor condition)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of all rounds:**

The champion (-3.17) remains the same 2-state machine. The scores are highly stochastic - the champion itself scored -0.85 in one round. The "true" expected score seems to be around -3 for the best configurations.

**What we've learned:**
- The core topology (GoToColor yellow → GoAwayColor yellow) is robust
- State 0: 4 transitions with GRAY(0.13), random(0.22), neighbors>7(w=14), neighbors>6(w=17) is optimal
- State 1: 2 transitions (GRAY 0.95, random 0.35) is the champion, but adding a 3rd/4th transition sometimes helps and sometimes hurts
- neighbors>5(w=12) in state 1 helped in round 5 (-3.12) but was worse in round 7
- The 4-transition variant with WHITE(0.80) was best this round (-2.70) but still worse than champion
- The stochasticity means we need to re-evaluate the champion topology itself

**Key observation:** The champion has been evaluated fewer times than variants. The variants consistently score around -2.5 to -3.1, while the champion scored -3.17 once. The true mean might be similar.

**Strategy:**
- Keep champion (elitism)
- Re-test the champion topology with slight variations to confirm robustness
- Try wider parameter exploration: different gray probabilities, different random probabilities
- Try completely different state 1 behavior (e.g., Exploration instead of GoAwayColor)
- Try 3-state machine with the champion core + a recovery state
- Explore different clr values (clr=5,6) for state 0

- One or two exploration variants with different topologies

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 12 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.42 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
